1998. MARY DAVIS was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of October, 1 blanket, value 4s., the goods of Ann Tarlizzic, widow.
ANN TARLIZZIC. I am a widow, and live in Golden-lane. The prisoner is a stranger; on the 3d of October I found this blanket on the landing-place - she had been up-stairs, and taken it from a room, which was not occupied.
ROBERT TIPPETT. I am the prosecutrix's grandson; I saw the prisoner sitting on the landing-place, on this blanket; she asked me for some person whom I did not know: I made no answer, and went into a back-room, and shortly after she asked how long the person who lodged in the front room would be; I said, "I could not tell;" she shut the door - I heard her go down soon after; I looked after her, and saw this blanket fall from her on the landing-place; I asked her what business she had to take it - she said she had not taken it, that she kicked it on the stairs, but I had seen her sitting on it; an officer was fetched, and took her.
SARAH GABLE. I lodge in the house; I heard a noise, opened the door, and Tippett said, "This woman has taken a blanket."
JOHN TWEEDY. I am an officer. I took her in charge.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was not in any room - I went on an errand to a man's wife there; it laid on the bannisters and as I turned round, it fell down.
GUILTY. Aged 34.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18261026-207]
FOURTH DAY. MONDAY, OCTOBER 30.
Fourth Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1998. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of October, 1 watch, value 6l.; 1 watch-key, value 4s.; 2 seals, value 6d.; 3 sovereigns; 1 half-crown; 14 shillings; and 1s. sixpence, the property of William Mann; 1 watch, value 1l.; 2 seals, value 10s.; 1 watch-key, value 4s., and 1 watch-chain, value 6d., the goods of Richard Bowden.
RICHARD BOWDEN. I am an officer of the Customs. I was on board the Atalanta on the 12th of October; she was lying off Irongate, St. Catherine's, near the Tower - I was awoke by a person taking my watch from off a locker, near to where I was lying - I had put it there the night before - I got out of bed, and saw a person going up the companion-ladder - I followed, saw him go to the adjoining vessel, and called Stop thief! I did not overtake him, but when I got to that vessel, I found my watch in one place and the case in another, on the deck - I saw the prisoner in about a minute in custody.
WILLIAM MANN. I am master of the Atalanta. Bowden slept on board that night - I heard an alarm, and saw him find his watch and case - I also missed a watch, three sovereigns, and some silver, from the state-room; I went and found the prisoner in a boat alone in about five minutes, and charged him with it - he denied having been on board, but said, he was smuggling two bottles of gin, and was pursued by the police; that he belonged to a vessel in the tier above; he was without a hat - a person brought it to him, and he owned it - as I was going down into the boat where he was, somebody said, "Captain, he has hove your watch overboard;" he said the man was a liar.
JOSEPH HARDING. On the morning of the 12 of October, I took the prisoner in this boat, about four vessels from the Atalanta - he said he went there to sleep, and that he had been let out of prison the night before - I found 1s. 4d. on him.
THOMAS HAMMOND. I am a Custom-house officer, and was on board the next vessel to the Atalanta - I heard an alarm, went on deck, and saw Bowden pick up his watch; I saw a person getting into the next vessel and followed him four or five vessels off into a boat - some men in the sixth vessel called out, "He is in a boat" - I got down and callared him; it was the prisoner - he put his hand into his pocket and took out something - then put his hand into the water and dropped it - I cannot tell what it was - he said he was pursued by two officers, as he had had two bottles of gin, and lost his hat in the scuffle - I said that was a lame story, and it was likely he had taken the watch - he said I was a d - d liar - I then went and found his hat near the companion door of the Atalanta - I brought it to him, put it on, and it fitted him very well; I said, "I have brought your hat;" he said, "Oh! have you;" I believehim to be the man whom I saw crossing the ships - it was between two and three o'clock in the morning.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. A countryman promised me two bottles of gin - I went on board to get it - I was pursued by an officer, and escaped across the tier, and in a quarter of an hour, they came and said I had robbed them - my hat had fallen into the water.
GUILTY. Aged 26.
Transported for Seven Years.

1999. JAMES BENNETT was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of September, 12lbs. of candles, value 6s., the goods of Thomas Birke, his master.
Mr. LAW conducted the prosecution.
JOHN FARRELL. I am servant to Thomas Birke, a tallowchandler - the prisoner was in his employ. On the 2d of September, about a quarter past nine o'clock at night, I saw him take an apron full of candles out of a box, and take them to his own premises - I gave information to Bain, the foreman, and he was afterwards apprehended - he had 12lbs. or perhaps 18lbs.; he took them from a place where candles are laid out for the men, who work at night; I was in the melting-house; he took a candle to take them.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How long after this was he in Mr. Birke's employ? A. Eight or ten days - Bain said, "Why did not you stop him" - I said I could not; he said, "Let it lay, and we will catch him again;" he was taken to the office on the 14th and discharged, because I was not there - he did not give himself up on this charge.
COURT. Q. How far was his house off? A. Not twenty yards - I went up-stairs and told Bain directly.
JOHN BAIN. Farrell informed me of this; I told him to he quiet about it - the prisoner did not surrender himself - I went to his house and found 6lbs. or 8lbs. of candles on the 14th - they were such as we make.
Cross-examined. Q. Why did not you go insantly and search his house? A. I had no authority; I believe there has been a charge against my master for smuggling, but not against me - I have no idea of the prisoner being a witness against my master; I do not expect it - the prisoner is married, and has one child.
RICHARD CORFF. I apprehended the prisoner on the 14th of September, on a charge of stealing soap - I went to his house, and found a quantity of candles in a box, they were so broken, I have not brought them - he was discharged about the soap; I took him afterwards about the candles.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he give himself up? A. He would, I believe - he sent me word that he would give himself up, but he had been absent.
Prisoner. I never took a candle; the officer let me remain at home all night, on promising to come in the morning, which I did - all the candles I had, these men gave me - Bain has called me up twice to work after the Excise officer came round.
COURT to JOHN BAIN. Q. Where does Mr. Birke live? A. In Fulham-road, about three-hundred yards froca the premises; I thought it not worth while to go and tell him; he was at home - I told his son of it - the prisoner never bought any candles; the men are not allowed candles - they were broken candles for the men's use.
NOT GUILTY.

2000. JAMES BENNETT was again indicted for stealing, on the 14th of September, 6lbs. of soap, value 8s., the goods of Thomas Birke.
JOHN BAIN. On the 8th of September I was in the factory, and heard a noise in the loft - no one has any business there; I had shut it an hour before; I called out,"Who is there?" but received no answer - I ran up-stairs, and met the prisoner coming out, with a box lid in his hand - I said, "What do you want? why not take a light?" he said, "A box-lid, to cover a box;" I looked round, and under a box saw two cakes of soap, in another box - I locked up the place, and when Mr. Birke came in the morning I told him; I then put a crooked nail into the soap, and left it there - it had been moved from the cutting-room. On the 14th it was missed, and I went with the officer to the prisoner's house, and saw him find two cakes of soap in his bureau - on cutting it the crooked nail was in it; I told the officer about the nail.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you state all this to Mr. White, the Magistrate? A. Yes, and he was discharged, because the witness was not there; he was committed by Mr. Hall, at Bow-street. I am not in the habit of calling at his house; Farrell used to call him up in the morning.
RICHARD CORFF. I apprehended the prisoner - he said he had no soap, and knew nothing of it; I found it in the bureau in his room, and the nail was in it; Bain had said he had put one into it - the bureau was not locked.
Prisoner's Defence. A box of fat stood in the yard; the carpenter sent me for a box lid - I went for it in the dark; the doors were open. Bain called out, "Who is there?" I said, "Me;" the soap had been there for months - Mr. Birke said he had lost none.
RICHARD CORFF. Mr. Birke said he would not swear to it, but left it to Bain.
GUILTY. Aged 25.

2001. WILLIAM POWELL, RICHARD EDWARDS, and JOHN HARRIS, alias WALKER , were indicted for stealing, on the 6th of October, 1 comb, value 1s. 10d., the goods of George Hicks.
GEORGE WADDINGTON. I am a constable of Bow-street. Harris was at the end of the counter; they came out across the way, and went a little distance - I crossed over, and pushed Edwards and Powell into a shop, at the corner of Henry-street - Mills brought in Harris, and I found this comb on him - also some stamps, and two wafer-stamps.
RICHARD MILLS. I was with Waddington - his account is correct.
MARY DICKINS. I am shopwoman to Mr. George Hicks. Powell and Edwards came in, and asked for a comb for graining, and said it must be three inches and a half long; I showed them some, but none would do; in about seven minutes, while they were there, Harris came in, and said he wanted a brush for graining; I went to look for some, but not finding them readily, he said he would call in the morning, between nine and ten o'clock - he did not appear to know the others; they all went out; Powell had a rule, and measured the comb. In a quarter of an hour the officer came in - I then missed a comb. They had taken up several in the shop, and I missed one then.
Cross-examined by MR. ANDREWS. Q. Had you counted them before? A. No; we sell a good many such. I believe this to be the one I missed.
ANN HICKS. I live with my father. I was out at the time, but this comb has my writing on it.
Cross-examined. Q. Are all your combs marked so? A. Yes - if it was sold the ink would still be there.
POWELL'S Defence. I went to buy a comb - they had none: I took nothing.

2002. JOHN GAWTHORNE and JOHN SMITH were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of October, 7 pieces of leather, value 1l. 15s., the goods of George Waite.
CHARLES HERDSFIELD. I am an officer. On the 7th of October, between-nine and ten o'clock, I was in Whitecross-street; I saw the two prisoners together - Gawthorne said to Smith, "To-morrow, mind; ten o'clock;" I said to Gawthorne, "What have you got there?" he said,"Some leather" - I asked him where he got it; he said, from Mr. Allen, of Green-arbour-court, Old Bailey, and that Mr. Armstrong had given him 30s. to fetch it and pay for it - I said, "You are not fetching it for yourself;" he said, No. I brought him to Giltspur-street.
DANIEL FORRESTER. I was with Herdsfield, and took Smith.
JOHN GOODWIN. I am in the employ of Mr. George Waite, a currier, of No. 16, Gray's-inn-lane. This is the same leather I saw at the examination, but I have seen so many pieces, I should not like to swear to them - I did swear to this piece, but there is no mark upon it; there was a piece of cordovan missing like this, but all shoulders are alike; I said this piece was my master's property, but I had no more reason for knowing it then than I have now - I have seen so many pieces like it since, that it makes me doubtful. Here is a mark on those other six pieces; I have no doubt but they were my master's; but the same person who marked these works for other shops.
COURT. Q. You say in your deposition, "One of these seven pieces of leather, being a neck of cordovan, I saw on my master's premises, and I know it to be his property, and form the marks on the other pieces, I believe them to be his property" - what is your belief now? A. I believe it is.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You have stated that you swore to one piece of leather; is there your master's mark on it? A. No, my master never marks leather - here is the tanner's mark on it; there was a finger-mark on this piece which I swore to, but it is not here now - I only believe them to be his, and cannot say they have not been sold.
GEORGE WAITE. I believe these seven pieces of leather to be mine; I have no doubt of it - I cannot say I never sold them, but I believe not. The prisoner was my apprentice, and had access to it (looking at his deposition) - I swore positively to them before the Magistrate, as that was my impression; there are marks on it made by a person who is now dead.
NOT GUILTY.

2003. THOMAS EDWARDS and JOSEPH NORRIS were indicted for stealing, on the 23d of September, one 5l. note, the property of Mary Edwards, from her person.
MARY EDWARDS. I live in Fashion-court, Spitafields. On the 23d of September my daughter gave me a 5l. note to redeem some pledges - I went to the Golden Anchor public-house, Golden-lane, for change, as the pawnbroker had none - Mrs. Jones, the landlady, returned me the note, as she had no change; I went away with it, and returned again soon after, and held it up for change at the bar, when some person behind me twisted it out of my hand - I was frightened, and cannot swear to any one - it was the same note my daughter had given me.
MARGARET TOMLINSON. I gave Edwards, who is my mother, a 5l. note, which my brother had given me - it was a new note - he had been paid off the day before; I have not seen it since.
SOPHIA FORDHAM. I live in Golden-lane. I was at the Golden Anchor on an errand, and saw the prosecutrix come for change for a 5l. note - she held it up in her hand - Norris went behind her, and snatched it out of her hand - Edwards was close behind him; they both ran away together; I am certain of their persons; I knew them both well before, and have no doubt whatever of them - I ran after them, but they got out of my sight - I did not call Stop thief! as I thought I should catch them, and knew I should know them; I knew their names - they have lived in the neighbourhood a long time - I told my father their names directly I got home.
MARGARET JONES. I keep the public-house. Edwards came on the 23d of September for change for a 5l. note; I could not get it as it was up-stairs, and I had no one to mind the bar; she went out, and came in again, and two or three customers came in at the same time; she held the note up with her finger and thumb, and a bit of the note out; she was standing with her back to the door, and a person, in a light coloured jacket, came in, and snatched it out of her hand; but it was neither of the two prisoners; I do not know the man who took it, but it was neither of them - I did not see Sophia Fordham there - I have a strong suspicion who it was - I saw him walking from the door; there were not two of them; there was only one - I do not know whether Fordham was there - I did not see any girl run out and give an alarm; the first thought that struck me was, that it was her son who took it; I have seen the two prisoners before - they have beenat my house - Fordham lives in the neighbourhood; her father is an officer; I believe the prisoners lived in the neighbourhood - I am not acquainted with their friends - I did not tell the Magistrate that I had some suspicion of a person; but I have seen a chap since brought up for some offence; I do not know what, and have now some suspicion of a person.
COURT. Q. How was the man dressed? A. He had a light coloured coat on; I have called it a jacket - I did not notice which it was.
SOPHIA FORDHAM re-examined. Q. How were they dressed? A. Norris had corded breeches and a corded jacket, of a dark colour - Edward had a kind of sailor's shirt, a light coloured jacket, and a cap - my father and Harrison took the prisoners - I was close to them; I swear positively they are the men, they were close together.
JAMES MASON. I am a publician. I changed a 5l. note on the 23d of October, but it was not to either of the prisoners.
JAMES FORDHAM. I am an officer. I apprehended Norris that evening from my daughter's information - she described him - I have known him for years. I took Edwards about twelve o'clock next day; he had a blue coat on. Norris was in a corderoy jacket and trowsers; we took him to the watch-house; he was talking to some woman there, and said there had been a to-do about a 5l. note at the bottom of Golden-lane. I know they frequented the Golden Anchor public-house all day long, and have moved them from there a hundred times - Edwards had changed his jacket for a coat.
THOMAS HARRISON. I was with Fordham, and took Norris - Edwards was taken next morning; he had a blue coat on; I had seen Norris at the Golden Anchor scores of times; Edwards had changed his light coat for a blue one with a man named Bone.
NORRIS' Defence. I was at the house having a pint of beer; Fordham came in; I said to Mrs. Jones, "What does he say?" he said there was a 5l. note lost; "Is there?" said I, but no one took me; as I returned from the play, Harrison dragged me into the watch-house.
EDWARD'S Defence. I was at the corner of my own court; one Bone came and said, "Will you sell me your coat;" I said, No - he said, "Let me try it one;" he did, and then went into a public-house to light his pipe - he came out with a 5l. note in his hand, and said, "I have a 5l. note;" he ran against me, and ran away, leaving me his coat to put on.
EDWARDS - GUILTY. Aged 17.
NORRIS - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Seven Years.

2005. RICHARD JACKSON was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of September, 2 planes, value 7s., and 1 saw, value 2s., the goods of George Kitchener; 2 saws, value 14s.; 2 planes, value 5s.; 1 axe, value 3s.; 1 square, value 1s.; 1 bevil, value 1s.; 2 gimblets, value 8d.; 1 basket, value 1s.; 2 centre-bits, value 1s., and 1 chalkline, value 1s., the goods of Thomas Newberry; 1 saw, value 4s.; 1 plane, value 3s., and 1 square, value 1s., the goods of James Barnett.
GEORGE KITCHENER. I am a carpenter and joiner. I left these tools safe, at half-past five o'clock on the night of the 13th of September, in a hoard by the Duke of York's premises, in the Green-park, and missed them next morning - I believe the shed was locked. The prisoner was a stranger.
EDWARD HEDDINGTON. I am a constable. On the 14th of September I saw Kitchener in Lawrence's shop, New-cut, Lambeth, where he identified a plane; I went into the back parlour, and found a basket containing the tools in the indictment, among others. I found the prisoner in custody of a patrol. I returned to the shop with a warrant, and found some more property.
THOMAS NEWBERRY. I am a carpenter. Some of these tools are mine.
THOMAS TRIBE. I am a carpenter, and work at the Duke of York's house. On the morning of the 14th of September I went to the Rose and Crown public-house; Kitchener said he had lost his tools; I went to the Newcut with him - he found some of his tools at Lawrence's; I saw Redman with some tools in his hand, offering them for sale; Kitchener's name was on them; he took me to the prisoner, at the Three Compasses public-house; McKear, the officer, was fetched, and found thirteen duplicates of tools on him.
JAMES McKEAR. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and found thirteen duplicates on him - one of which relates to this property - I found a small tool on him; I asked his name - he said, they called him anything, and he lived anywhere.

WILLIAM REDMAN. [trialAccount t18261026-215]
2006. JACOB EVANS was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Camp Collins, by administering to him 6ozs. of decoction of fox-glove, well knowing the same to be a deadly poison.
JOHN BURBIDGE. I am a cabinet-maker, and live in Bayne's-row, Clerkenwell - Camp Collins was my apprentice; he died last Thursday morning, about half-past ten o'clock; he was seventeen years old; he had been ailing at intervals for some months; he complained for weeks together of a giddiness in his head, which at times seized him suddenly when at work - he was unable to work for three or four weeks - I have known the prisoner about three years, and I believe he was lately been out of employ - he published a book on the use of herbs some months ago - he called on me last Tuesday, about eleven o'clock, and told me Collins' mother had spoken to him, to know if he could prepare anything from herbs, to prevent this disorder, and he had promised to call and see the boy, and asked how he was afficted - the boy replied, "I feel pretty well now;" "He said, how do you feel when the disease attacks you?" he said, "I am taken with a stupid giddiness, and swimming my head, as if something was running all about me;" the prisoner passed, and then said, "If your mother will get two or three pennyworth of fox-glove at the herbalist, and put to that one quart of water, and boil it till it is reduced to something more than half a pint" - I said, "Mr. Evans, is it to be between half a pint and a pint?" he said Yes - this the boy I believe perfectly understood; the prisoner said, "And then, my boy, you will take half that quantity; it will make you very sick, and work you upwards and downwards" - that he would be in that way for for several hours, and probably not able to work all day - I went down-stairs with the prisoner; and the boy followed us, and said, "Sir, if you have no objection, I had rather Mr. Evans get these things himself, as I do not know where to get them, and my mother will satisfy him;" Evans said, "Very well, the expense will be very few pence;" he then went away, and called again next day (Wednesday) about eleven o'clock - he came into the shop, and said, "Now, my boy, I have brought the stuff, I should like you to take it in something large enough to hold half of it" - the boy fetched up a large breakfast cup, which held something less than half a pint - Evans filled it about three parts full, and said, "Now, my boy, you must not sip this, it is bitter and nasty, you must drink it off at once;" the boy took it from him, and drank it off at a draught - he had some bread and cheese, which he was eating for a lunch, and he eat some very hastily to take the laste out - Evans said, "If this does not operate in about half an hour, take about the same quantity again;" and he said,"You will see, Mr. Burbidge, that he takes it, if it does not begin to operate about that time;" I looked at the watch which hung up, and said, "It is now such a time, and at such a time, the other must be administered;" Evans had not left the shop till the half hour expired - the boy then untied his apron, began to rub himself, and then went down to the yard; he returned in a short time - Evans had then left the shop; the boy continued to work for about a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes - I conceived the stuff had taken its effect, and did not give him the second dose - the boy then complained of being very sick, and went into the next room, which was his bed-room, and vomitted a good deal - I went to him in about ten minutes, and said, "Can you come down to dinner?" he said, "I fear, I cannot eat I am so sick;" he came down, saw the dinner, but went up directly, saying he was so sick. When I returned from dinner, I asked how he was? he said, "Very sick" - he had then taken the medicine and hour and a half - he took it exactly at ten minutes to twelve o'clock, and it was twenty minutes past when he went into the yard - he still complained of being very sick, but brought very little up, except some green yellow stuff - I went to him several times, found him very sick, and advised him to walk about, as it would operate better - at four o'clock, I asked him down to tea; he said he had rather have it sent up - it was sent up; he took some, but eat nothing - a little before five o'clock, he came down into the kitchen, drew a chair to the fire, and leaned his head on the table, and at short intervals vomited - my wife advised him to take some warm water, which he declined; I said I thought he had better take the other half, but he did not. I came down about seven o'clock, and thought he was asleep; I said to my wife he had better not sleep there; he said,"I am not asleep, I had rather sit by the fire, as I feel so sick" - I then went out and found his parents at a chapel, told them of it, and said I thought he had not taken sufficient; they advised me to persuade him to take the rest; I went home and found my wife had just put him to bed; she went up to him a little before ten o'clock, he appeared asleep, and she brought the light down - a man named Harris went up about a quarter to eleven, and he thought him asleep; he slept in the next bed - I went up about ten minutes to seven o'clock in the morning, and he said he was very sick every now and then, but in no pain except in his head, putting his hand to his forehead - I sent for Mr. Whitmore, and about half-past ten o'clock the boy died. He was opened next morning, in my presence - there were four surgeons. The prisoner had never said how much the two or three pennyworth of fox-glove would produce - the bottle was about three-parts full; he did not give him quite half; here is the bottle, with the remainder of the stuff in it.
MR. HENRY WHITMORE. I am a surgeon, and live in Coldbath-square - I have been practising for seventeen or eighteen years. I was called in about half-past seven o'clock last Thursday morning, and found the boy labouring under symptoms common to vegetable nancatle poison, and under some symptoms peculiar to a preparation of fox-glove - his pulse was slow, weak, and very irregular; his extremities cold, and the pupil of the eye ceased to obey the rays of light - I had the room darkened, called for a candle, and found the pupil would not contract and dilate. I was told he had taken fox-glove, and the symptoms agreed with that; they could not have deceived me if they had tried, as he had taken such an immense quantity - I ordered him braudy every ten minutes, gave him medicine, and ordered him blisters; but he died while they were preparing. I afterwards opened his body, and there was an absence of all disease that might have produced death. Death is produced by vegetable poison, not by occasioning disease, but by lessening the energy of the nervous system, so that the heart and all the energies cease to act. I have no doubt the decoction of fox-glove caused his death; it is a medicine which is admiaistered in similar cases. The boy had been under my care for two months, and I had repeatedly administered it to him, properly guarded; it was a case in which I judged it right - I administered it to him successfully, I think, as his health was restored, and he returned to work. I cannot say how much would be sold for 2d. or 3d.; the prisoner told me, in the Coroner's Jury-room, that he had boiled down 4 ozs., which was a greater quantity than I thought - from measuring the cup, the boy must have taken about 51/2 ozs. of the stuff, as 61/2 ozs. are left in the bottle, which makes 12 ozs., which 4 ozs. would produce. It is impossible such a quantity could be given without danger; from calculation, I find the quantity of decoction twenty-four times the strength our College orders us to make, and then he had given him six times the quantity beyond our extreme dose, and twelve or fifteen times the quantity beyond the common dose. Nothing but complete ignorance could have led to such a dose being given - in my opinion, it must have produced death - I should have thought five or seven drops of this mixture a proper dose. I have seen the book the prisoner has published, and am sorry to say, it will produce much mischief, if acted upon. I remember a case where an undue and uncertain quantity of fox-glove was given, and was unexpectedly beneficial, but I do not know how much.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18261026-216]
2007. JOHN TIFF was indicted for killing and slaying Daniel Baker ,
MR. ALLEY conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM SNOW. I am a waterman. On the 30th of September I had brought a lady from a ship, with a quantity of luggage - she was landing at Blackwall-stairs; the prisoner was there, acting as a porter; Baker came up, and said to the lady, "Do you want me, ma'am;" she said to me, "Yes; give the chest and hammock to that tall man;" he came to the boat, took hold of the rope, and the prisoner struck him the first blow - they put themselves in a fighting attitude - Baker fell on the gunwale of a boat, in the third round - I heard him say, Oh! but they fought two more rounds afterwards, and Baker died in about twenty minutes.
WILLIAM JONES PERCEVAL. I am a surgeon. I was fetched to the deceased, and found him dying - after his death I opened his body, and found five or six pints of extravasated blood in the belly; there were several external bruises, and one corresponding with the rupture of the spleen - he died from the extravasation of blood occasioned by that rupture - the spine had been diseased before.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Would his fighting occasion it? A. Not without receiving a blow.
JAMES LEWIS. I saw this man after his death - the surgeon saw the same person.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you present when he examined it? A. No; he was carried to the King's Arms public-house, and laid on the table; there was no other dead man there - I did not search the house.
Prisoner's Defence (written). I called out to know if the lady wanted a porter to carry the things on shore; Daniel Baker also plied her; she nodded, and on the boat reaching the shore the waterman called to me, to pull her in, which I did. I then took hold of the hammock, which the deceased perceiving, attempted to take from me; and in so doing, he pushed me into the water. I got out of the water, and, in the irritation of the moment, struck him, which was returned, and we fought four or five rounds. He being older than myself, I rather endeavoured to keep him off, than to strike him; but in the last round he unfortunately fell on the nose of the boat, which hurt the spine and caused his death. I had no animosity whatever towards the deceased: indeed I hardly knew him.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18261026-217]
2008. MARTHA BYATT was indicted for that she, on the 30th of September, at St. George, Hanover-square, 12 pieces of false and counterfeit milled money and coin, each and every of them made and counterfeited to the likeness and similitude of a piece of good, lawful, and current milled money and silver coin of the realm, called a shilling; the same counterfeited and milled money, nor either of them not being then cut in pieces, unlawfully and feloniously did put off to one George Dutton at a lower rate and value than they by their denomination did import and were counterfeited for, that is to say, for the sum of 4s., against the statute.
2d COUNT, the same, only stating that the said counterfeited pieces of milled money were put off at a lower rate, &c. that is to say, for four pieces of the current coin of this realm, called shillings, of the value of 1s. each, against the statute.
MR. BOLLAND conducted the prosecution.
GEORGE DUTTON. I have known the prisoner from my childhood - eighteen years - in consequence of a communication from Limbrick, I had some interview with her, and agreed for her to meet me, on the 30th of September, at the Green Man public-house, Green-street, Grosvenor-square - she came - I saw her three or four doors from the house - I went in, and asked for a glass of cold gin and water - I then saw her come up to the house, and told Limbrick she was at the door; Lee then searched me in Limbrick's presence; I had nothing about me; Lee then gave me four shillings, which Limbrick marked - I put them into my pocket; I went to the prisoner, and asked if she had any little ones, meaning sixpences - I then said I wanted two dozen big ones, meaning shillings - she said, "You aregoing to break me at once" - I said, "No; I mean one dozen of big ones;" which she gave me; I put them into my pocket, and gave her the four shillings, which I had in my pocket - I went in, and in a few minutes, she was brought into the parlour and searched; I was also searched.
Prisoner. Q. Did I agreed to meet you that night? A. Your daughter did; you had agreed to meet me on the 25th of September, but did not come; and your daughter agreed for you to meet me on the 30th.
JOHN LIMBRICK. I am an officer of Hatton-garden. In consequence of information, which I received about the prisoner, I gave directions to Dutton; Lee and Kirby were in company with me. On the 30th of September, about a quarter to nine o'clock at night, at the Green Man; Dutton came into the house, and said, "They are in sight" - Lee then searched him - he had nothing about him; Lee gave him four shillings, which I had marked - he went out, and soon after returned. saying, "It is all right;" I then saw the prisoner brought in - she was searched, and the four marked shillings, which had been given to Dutton, were found on her - I searched Dutton, and found these twelve counterfeit shillings on him (producing them) - he said in her presence, that he got them from her; she exclaimed, "Oh! you traitor! Oh! Mr. Limbrick, I am a dead woman."
WILLIAM LEE. I was with Limbrick on this evening; he gave me four marked shillings, which I gave Dutton, after seeing that he had nothing about him - I went out shortly after him, and saw the prisoner near him - I took her, brought her in, and I found on her the four shillings, which I had given to Dutton; twelve shillings were found on him.
WILLIAM KIRBY. I have heard the officer's evidence - it is correct.
JOSEPH NICHOL. I am moneyer of the Mint. These shillings are counterfeit, and from one die - they do not appear to have been in circulation, but they have been prepared for circulation.
JOHN FIELD. I am an inspector of counterfeit coin. - The shillings are counterfeit - they have been rubbed down with scowering-paper, and then coloured with something, to make them look black, as if they had been in circulation.
Prisoner's Defence. I have had twelve children - my husband is a painter and glazier, but neglected his business; I have been left in distress, and for a week together have been without a bit of meat; I have been brought to this through a person who owed me some rent, and gave me seven score of these - I kept them by me a long time, and wanted bread while I had them. I have often returned, after going out to pass them, and pawned something instead; it was nothing but distress led me to do it.
GUILTY.
The prisoner was called upon to receive judgment, and upon praying benefit of clergy, a counter-plea was put in, on behalf of the Crown, and a record of the conviction of Martha Byatt read, for having put off, on the 25th of February, 1819, seven counterfeit shillings, to Cecilia Willis, for 2s. 71/2d. - the prisoner pleaded that she was not the same person, upon which a Jury were impannelled to try the issue.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL. I am assistant to the solicitor of the Mint. I produce a copy of the record of the conviction of Martha Byatt, at the April Session, 1819; I examined it with the original - it is correct - (read).
BARNARD GLEED. I am an officer. I know the prisoner, and knew her in 1819 - I was present at the April Session that year; the prisoner is the person who was tried and convicted here - I was a witness on that occasion, and gave evidence against her.
GEORGE BRAND. I was a turnkey at the House of Correction in 1819; the prisoner was in my custody twelve months from that time - I am certain of her person.
The Jury found that she was the same person, and she accordingly received sentence of Death.

2009. ELIZA BYATT was indicted for a like offence, in putting off, on the 27th of September, six counterfeit shillings; and the said MARTHA BYATT was indicted for that she, before the said felony was committed, unlawfully did counsel, aid, abet, and procure the said Eliza Byatt, to do and commit the said felony, in manner and form aforesaid, against the statute.
GEORGE DUTTON. I had seen Martha Byatt on the 25th of September, and agreed to meet her on the 27th, at a person's house, near North-row; she promised she would have some little ones - I went to the place, but she was not there; the prisoner came in the evening, and sent for me; I went out, found her in Green-street, and said, "Have you got any little ones?" she said, No, but she had got six big ones, meaning counterfeit shillings, which I took of her, and gave her two goods shillings, which Limbrick had given to me, on the Tuesday before - I kept the bad shillings till I saw Limbrick, and gave them to him.
Prisoner ELIZA BYATT. Q. Did I say I came from my mother? A. Yes; she did not say she brought them on her own account.
JOHN LIMBRICK. I am an officer. In consequence of information I gave directions to Dutton, and gave him two shillings, which were not marked; I afterwards received from him six counterfeit shillings, which I produce - they were wrapped in paper, as they are now; Kirby was with me when I apprehended Eliza.
JOHN FIELD. These are counterfeit shillings, and have never been in circulation.
ELIZA BYATT's Defence. My mother never brought me to this - it is I that has brought her to it.
NOT GUILTY.

[trialAccount t18261026-226]
2017. THOMAS SKINNER, WILLIAM MORRIS, ELIZABETH GASCOWEN, and MARY BODEN were indicted for stealing, on the 29th of September, 1 trunk, value 6s.; 20 table-cloths, value 15l. 10s. 6d.; 20 pillow-cases, value 3l. 10s.; 6 bolster-cases, value 1l. 4s.; 30 towels, value 2l. 5s.; 9 cloths, value 2l.; 2 pieces of linen, value 9s.; 10 yards of dimity, value 10s.; 5 yards of cotton, value 10s.; 2 coverlids, value 6s.; 1 shawl, value 5s., and 4 napkins, value 14s.; the goods of William Bowie.
ELIZABETH BOWIE. I am the wife of William Bowie. On Friday the 29th of September we removed from Burr-street to Crombie-row, Mile-end Old-town; I had a trunk containing the articles stated in the indictment; I saw it put into the van a little before seven o'clock at night - here is a table-cloth and towel, which I can swear to.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you any mark upon them? A. My husband is a table-linen manufacturer - it is our own manufacture, and was the only one that was in the trunk of this pattern; I washed it the day before I put it into the trunk - it was marked E. B., but the the mark is picked out; I have sold a great many of that pattern; I know the napkin by the loop - that is not a general thing; I have seen some so, but few people take the trouble of doing it - here is a hole in one corner, by which I know it.
JOHN HENRY BOWIE. I am the prosecutor's son. I assisted in removing the goods - Morris had asked me if Iwanted a van; I said, No, I had engaged one; I went again to get it, and when I came back, Morris was there; I had not hired him - we told them to put the trunks first into the van, but this trunk was left to the last - Morris then wanted to put it at the back on the top, but we moved a heavy basket of crockery, and put it under it - they were then in a hurry to get loaded, and I said, I would give them a pot of beer - we passed by a public-house opposite the London-docks - I gave Morris 1s. to get a pot of beer there; I saw several people come out of that house, and pass on - the van then went on to Crombie's-row, which may be a mile or a mile and a half; I took a basket, containing some glasses and light things, and went into the house; I had told Morris not to take anything out till I had seen it was all clear - when I went in, this trunk was in the van, and not a rope was touched, but I cannot say whether the rope was attached to the trunk or not - when I came out, I could not see the trunk - the basket was on the ground - Morris was about the place; I jumped on the van, and, when I had been up about a second, a man said that a man had gone down a turning on the other side of the way with a trunk of ours; I called to some one to run after him - Morris heard me, but he stopped about two minutes, pulling off his jacket; he then ran a little way, but returned in about a minute, and said, "Where is the man that said the trunk was gone?" there was an alarm, and a number of people round the van, so that I was afraid to go away myself; this is the trunk which I missed; I found it the next day at Mr. Dunbar's, a broker; I had observed it on the van, when I left it to go into the house; I know that my father had this pattern of tablecloths about fourteen years ago, and they were sold to Lady Cooke, of Norfolk, and a table-cloth of this pattern has been in use in our family ever since.
EDWARD KING. I am a carman. I went to Burr-street to remove some goods, as I was going by the toll-gate near the London-docks; I saw Morris talking to the toll-man - he asked if I was going to Burr-street to load some furniture; I said, Yes - he asked if they would want any body to help - I said, I did not know; he said he had nothing to do, and had a great mind to go and see if he could get a job; he went in the van, and assisted to load the goods - this trunk came out last, and I was surprised at it, because I had given orders for the trunks to be placed in the bottom of the van - when we went to Crombie's-row Morris went with us, and he took this trunk out, and placed it on the ground; I asked him why he did not take it into the house; he said, the house was not ready; he then moved it to the side of the cart next the road; I asked him what he wanted with it; he said, To stand on it to reach a rope;" there was a rope that he might have wanted it for; and in a minute or two it was gone.
SARAH TURNER. I live in Albion-street. I saw Mary Boden bring this trunk with James Spencer into Elizabeth Gascowen's room, No. 30, Albion-street, on the evening of the 29th of September - it appeared loaded and heavy; I went into the room, and saw Elizabeth Gascowen light a candle - I went home, and left them there; in about twenty minutes Gascowan and Boden came to me, and Gascowen said there was a box in the alley, and told me to go and take it which I did - this is it; I am sure it is the same that I saw them take into Gascowen's room - when I went out, the lock was off, but it was lying on the top of it; I sold it the next day to Mr. Dunbar - on the Sunday evening, Gascowan offered to give me two napkins, but I would not have them.
JAMES DUNBAR. I keep a broker's shop. On the 30th of September I went to Albion-street, and bought this trunk of Sarah Turner.
HESTER SOLOMAN. Mary Boden came to my house, and asked me if I knew any body who wanted to buy half a dozen of table-cloths; I cannot say when it was.
JAMES LEE. I am an officer of Lambeth-street. I heard of the robbery; I went to Skinner's room, and found a table-cloth and towel - he and Gascowen were in bed together; I took Morris a few days after.
MORRIS' Defence. I went there to get a job - they agreed to give me 2s. 6d. to assist; I did not hear him tell me not to unload; I took the trunk to stand on, and while we were getting a chest of drawers off, the box was taken.
GASCOWEN's Defence. Spencer came up to me, and Boden, with the box, and said, "May I take this into your room till my landlord comes in?" he said it belonged to a sailor - he went out with a bundle; I have not seen him since; I found the table-cloth and napkin on the stairs.
BODEN's Defence. Turner said she would burn the name out, as she knew what Spencer was, and she thought it was not got honestly.
HANNAH BAKER. I live in Henry-street. Turner came to me, and said, if I would give her a gown and apron, she would not come here.
SARAH TURNER. It is not true.
NOT GUILTY.

ABRAHAM LEVISON. I live in Elder-street, Spitalfields, and am a working jeweller. 2019. EDWARD JARVIS was indicted for bigamy.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
JOHN GEORGE LEIGH. I am deputy parish clerk of St. George, Hanover-square. I have the marriage-register, by which it appears that on the 3d of July last, Edward Jarvis Tollast, widower, and Elizabeth Whitehurst, spinster, were married - it is witnessed by Hemry and myself - I do not remember the parties.
JOHN HEMRY. I witnessed this marriage - I remember the prisoner as the person married to Miss Elizabeth Whitehurst; she is now in court.
Cross-examined by MR. ANDREWS. Q. Were you a friend of the parties? A. No; I am a publican, and live opposite to the church-gate - Jarvis ordered a breakfast at my house for four or five persons, but only himself and Whiteburst came: after breakfast, he said he was going to be married, and was in a difficulty about nobody coming to be witnesses, and asked if I would go over, which I did; I knew neither of them before - I asked him where the lady lived; he said in Windmill-street. Tottenham-court-road - I have seen her several times since.
HANNAH GREY. Up to the 10th of August, I was a widow; on that day I married the prisoner at St. Mary, Lambeth, Surry.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you known him before? A. Yes, for two or three months; Miss Finlayson is my niece; He parted from me at Kennington about three days after we were married, and I lodged at a butcher's at Camden-town.
JAMES LEDGER. I apprehended the prisoner on a warrant for an assault, in Church-passage, St. Giles.
GUILTY. Aged 54.

2020. EDWARD JARVIS was again indicted for a like offence.
JOHN GEORGE LEIGH. I produce the marriage-register of St. George, Hanover-square, by which it appears that Edward Jarvis, bachelor, was married to Rachael Pearce, spinster, by license, on the 16th of December, 1797 - I was not in office at that time - the register is signed Thomas Ash, curate, and by the clerk and sexton; I knew their writing - the clerk and sexton are dead.
JOHN STRATTON. I know the prisoner; he came to work for me about six years ago - he came from Horncastle, in Lincolnshire; he sent for his wife, and she came up; I saw her, and have heard her say her maiden name was Rachael Pearce, many times, in his presence; they lodged in my house; she is now living in Clarendon-street, Somer's-town - she is the same person who lived with him, as his wife - they have a son about twenty-four years old, and a daughter older; they always treated them as their children; his wife is about sixty years old.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know where she is now? A. Yes; I saw her six weeks ago - he called her Rachael, and also wife, and she called him husband. The children called them mother and father.
ELIZABETH FINLAYSON. I am niece to Mrs. Gray. I obtained an extract of the register which has been produced. I saw Rachael Jarvis at Clarendon-street - I afterwards saw the prisoner behind the counter of a shop in Windmill-street - I cannot say he was serving there; I told him of his marriage to Rachael Pearce, of Clarendon-street - he at first denied it, but afterwards said she was his wife; I said, "How could you think of marrying my aunt, when you knew you were married?" he said he knew he was a scoundrel or villain, and said, "I know I am married, and my wife is a good manager, a good liver, and a good provider, but our tempers did not agree;" I said, "You have taken my aunt's money?" he said, "Yes, I have;" I said, "Now, take my advice, young as I am - take 100l. and fly." but he would not.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it by your advice your aunt prosecuted? A. Not exactly; I thought it my duty to interfere. I live with my mother. The prisoner and my aunt only lived together four or five days - she asked my advice about this; she is sixty-eight years old, and I am nineteen.
JOHN GEORGE LEIGH. I produce the register of the prisoner's marriage to Whitehurst, as I read before.
ELIZABETH WHITEHURST. This register is signed by me; the prisoner is the man I married at St. George, Hanover-square, on the 3d of July.
Prisoner's Defence. I am compelled to state that Pearce never was my wife, nor was I in London till about five years ago; I never acknowledged to Finlayson that Pearce was my wife - all our conversation was about my running away. She was married to a man named Jarvis, but that was not me; I had a cousin named Jarvis - he came to town, and he was Pearce's husband; he went to America - she was visiting in Kent - we became acquainted, and we cohabited - I have a son and daughter, but I am not her husband. Whitehurst was my first wife, and I am proud to own it.
CHARLES McDUFF. I know the prisoner, and know his hand-writing; I believe the signature to the marriage with Rachael Pearce to be his.
GUILTY. Aged 54.
Transported for Seven Years.

2022. MICHAEL PETERS and THOMAS DONOVAN were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of October, 1 pair of trousers, value 7s., the goods of John Kinder Cheese.

2024. WILLIAM ASHTON and RICHARD BAXENDEN were indicted for stealing, on the 22d of October, 2 brass weights, value 2s., the goods of Sarah Roberts, widow.
SARAH ROBERTS. I am a widow, and keep a chandler's shop, in St. John-street-road. On the morning of the 22d of October, the prisoners came into my shop, between six and seven o'clock, with another person - they asked for a roll and butter - I went to the kitchen for the butler - they paid me - Owen gave me information - I looked, and missed two weights, and have not found them.
MARY OWEN. I am Roberts' servant. I saw the prisoners come into the shop, and another standing just outside - I saw Ashton take the weights, and give them to the one outside - I locked the prisoners in the shop.
JOHN DAVIS. I received them in charge.
BAXENDEN's Defence. The woman said I had got them at first.
ASHTON - GUILTY. Aged 13.
Confined Fourteen Days and Whipped.
BAXENDEN - NOT GUILTY.

2027. WILLIAM PHILLIPS was indicted for embezzlement.
JANE HODDINOTT. My husband deals with Mr. Kemp. On the 2d of October, between nine and twelve o'clock in the morning, I paid the prisoner 1 sovereign, 7 shillings, and 2s. 3d. in copper, on account of Mr. Kemp - he wrote"Paid" on the bill.
MATHIAS KEMP. The prisoner was my servant for about eight months. On the 2d of October I sent him with some bread and a bill - he never brought me any of this money - it was his duty to pay it to me as soon as he returned - on the Tuesday following, I asked him about this bill; he said it was not paid.
JOHN TWEEDY. I apprehended him - he was very tipsy.
GUILTY. Aged 32.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18261026-237]
FIFTH DAY. TUESDAY, OCTOBER 31.
Third Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
2028. PHILIBERT MATHEY was indicted for that he on the 28th of June, feloniously did sell to Benjamin Lewis Valliamy, a piece of wroughplate of gold, called a watch-case, with a certainmark thereon of a crown, with the figures 18 instead of the mark of the Lion-passant, made with a skimp(used in pursuance of the statute) made by the Company of Goldsmiths, in London, to mark gold-plate, made and wrought of the standard of 18 carats of fine gold in every pound weight troy, which mark had been then before transposed, and removed from a certain other piece of wrought plate of gold, called a watch-case, he knowing that the said mark had been transposed and removed thereon, from the said other piece of wrought plate of gold, called a watch-case, against the statute.
FOUR other Counts varying the manner of laying the charge.
MESSRS. ALLEY and LAW conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM DIMES. I am one of the Assayers at Goldsmith's-hall. On the 11th of January, Frederick Humbert and the prisoner came there to enter their mark as partners - they signed their names, and their mark was F. H. and P. M. - I saw them sign their names, and they brought this stamp with them, on which we make an impression, and return it to them; they keep it; and from that mark, we know the plate that comes from them - we never stamp any gold which comes without the manufacturer's stamp, but detain it - they also send two tickets, stating the weight and description of the articles they send, with their name and address.
JEREMIAH FULLER. I belong to Goldsmith's-hall. On the 23d of June, I received three gold-cases and two dials; they were two double-bottoms, one single case, and two dials - two tickets came with them - here is one of the tickets; the cases were marked with the prisoner's mark - two of the cases came in the morning; we take a receipt when they have any property back - here is the receipt we had that day.
WILLIAM DIMES. To the best of my knowledge and belief, this receipt is the prisoner's writing.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you ever see him write, except when he put his name in the book? A. No; I stood by him at that time; I could not decide without a comparison - I have not received many tickets from him - I am in the habit of referring to his signature daily; I do not think I should be enabled to decide, that this is his writing without a comparison.
JEREMIAH FULLER re-examined. Q. Have you communicated with the prisoner on his writing? A. No; I have received these notes from him. On the 23d of June I weighed the plate sent to me, and delivered it to Mr. City, the market - he assayed them, and I delivered the cases to the prisoner's porter, who was in the continual habit of bringing things from him - on the 26th of June, I received two more cases by that porter, and Mr. City had them detained.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you ever see the prisoner in company with that porter? A. No; I only knew him as bringing things, which he represented were the prisoner's.
COURT. Q. Did the porter bring plate, which had the prisoner's mark on it, to have the hall-mark put on it? A. Yes.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Do you know whether that man was porter to any other house? A. No; the stamp might have been in the possession of other persons - all I know is what that porter told me, and by the mark - the prisoner's mark was the only mark he brought.
WILLIAM CITY. I am a drawer in the Assayer's-office, on the 23d of June I received from Mr. Fuller two double bottoms, one single case, and two dials; I drew them for the Assayer, and stamped them, and put several private marks on the wires and on the bottoms; I then gave them to Mr. Fuller, they had the initials of Mathey and Humbert on them. On the 26th I received one double-bottom case, and on examining it I found the wires of it had come on the 23d, but they had had other bottoms attached to them, they had got my private marks on them, and they were detained. I went with two officers to the prisoner's house, in Hyde-street, Bloomsbury, on the 27th of June; I found the prisoner there, dressed like the other men; I did not know him at that time; there was only one man there who had not his working dress on; I first asked for them to deliver up all the cases in the shop whatever state they were in. Some person in the shop showed me a tin box with several parts of watches in it, the prisoner was present, and conversed with me; I found in the tin box these three wires and these two loose bottoms, they have all my private mark on them, which I had put on them on the 23d - I then asked the prisoner to deliver up his books, and they were produced. I found in the book an entry of a watch-case sold to Mr. Vulliamy, in Pall-mall, and I said to the prisoner, "This is booked on the 28th, and this day is only the 27th," the prisoner said, "I know it, I have made that mistake;" he said he had sent it that morning to them, and had made that mistake. I then went to Mr. Vulliamy's, who was out of town - Mr. Thompson there produced to me a double-bottomed case, marked by the Company; the bottoms have got my private mark on them, but the wires have not. When those bottoms had come to me at the Hall, they had come in other wires, and I have not the smallest doubt that they had been transposed from them. I have compared the bottoms found at Vulliamy's with the wires found at the prisoner's, they have all got my private mark on them, and correspond exactly in all respects; here is a bill of parcels which Mr. Vulliamy delivered at the office - I have not said any thing to the prisoner about this bill; the outer bottom has the crown and F. No. 18 in the centre of it, on the left the leopard's head, and on the right the veriable letter denoting the year, which in this case is an I, and over in the maker's private mark; the middle bottoms have a crown and 18, and over it the stamp of the maker; it is what is called a double-bottomed hunter; the bottoms have my private mark, but not the wires; the three wires which I did mark were all found at the prisoner's.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. When the cases are sent to you, are they in a complete state? A. They have only to be polished; the cases always fit the watches before they come there to be marked; I know that is invariably the case from being brought up to the business. The prisoner is a foreigner; he speaks broken English; I saw him at the office the next day - I do not know that he has a brother there; I am sure it was the prisoner who spoke to me; I had some difficulty to understand him, but I am quite sure I did understand him; when I stated, "This is a mistake, this is entered the 28th, and this is but the 27th," his answer was, "I made a mistake." I will not swear positively whether he said, "The case has been sent to Mr. Vulliamy's," or "I have sent it." The rims are not in all cases marked with the hall-mark, but they are sometimes, they have myprivate mark on, which I cannot describe, because it would defent my purpose, but it is such a mark as would not be removed by polishing, nor without destroying the case. When I went to the shop, the prisoner and three others were in their working dress, and one not in a working dress.
ANDREW THOMPSON. I am foreman to Mr. Vulliamy, of Pall-mall; I have been there twelve years; during the life of Mr. Humbert my master dealt with him, then with the prisoner and him, and since his death with the prisoner only; I once saw the prisoner at work at Hyde-street, Bloomsbury; I have seen him there several times; I cannot tell when Mr. Humbert died; he left England several months ago, and I think has been dead about two months; I am certain he left England before June; I recollect Mr. City calling on me on the 27th of June; he asked for a case, I showed him one which I had received a bill of parcels with - this is the bill, I marked it at the time; I believe it is the prisoner's hand-writing, but I have never seen him write - I have received this hand-writing with things that came from his house.
COURT. Q. In the course of business you have dealt upon this hand-writing? A. We have no occasion for any writing but bills of parcels - they were in this writing; I have no doubt whatever it came from the prisoner.
MR. LAW. Q. I believe you made some hesitation is delivering them, and you gave them up at the office? A. Yes; I received the bill and the case from an crrand-man, who is employed by us and by others; he has been employed in bringing work from the prisoner to us for about a month - I cannot identify the prisoner's work individually, but the case is made in the same way that we order work to be made by that house; though the errand-man had only brought work from the prisoner about a month, I had known him eighteen months, his name is Nicholas.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. You say this is similar work to what you have received from Humbert's house for ten years past? A. No, the fashions vary, but it has been nearly similar to this for the last four or five years - it is such work as was made by Mr. Humbert's workmen before the prisoner came to this country; I cannot tell whose hand-writing this is; it might be made out by a clerk, but it is the same writing as comes from that house; the porter was employed by four other persons to my knowledge, and he might be by other persons; I took the case in myself from him, and partly opened it, and put it into a drawer; I can undertake to say that I showed the same case afterwards; three or four other persons might have access to the drawer, but I do not think they had; I put a watch into the case, and I could identify that botter than the case. I did not particularly examine this case; I examined one that came with it; no person has any right to go to that drawer but Mr. Vulliamy and myself; I believe the case I took to the office was the one I showed to Mr. City, I believe so from finding it in the drawer where I put it after he looked at it; ever since the illness of Mr. Humbert, we have uniformly received bills in this hand-writing.
GEORGE MILES. I am senior Assayer at Goldsmith's-hall, and have made assays of these bottoms and rings which came from Mr. Vullamy's; the rings are about 9s. or 10s. an ounce inferior to the bottoms.
The entry in the book and the bill of parcels were read as follows:- "June 28th, No. 24, a hunting case, double bottom." - 12l. 18s.
COURT. Q. Not only the date, but the article and the amount correspond in the book and on the bill? A. Yes.
WILLIAM REED. I am a constable of Hatton-garden. I was applied to from Goldsmith's-hall to go to the prisoner in Hyde-street; while we were taking him to the office he asked what he was taken for? I said, "I suppose you know more about it than we do, for we seldom have such things," he said, "For lowering gold?" I did not know the prisoner myself, but I believe he told his name to Mr. City.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. He spoke in broken English? A. Yes - I will not swear he said,"Was it for lowering gold," or "I suppose it is for lowering gold;" he said, "What am I taken for;" I said,"You know more than we." and he said, "I suppose for lowering gold," or "for lowering gold."
ANDREW LLOYD. I ansisted in taking the prisoner - I found him in his working dress - he asked what he was taken for; Reed said, "You know more than we do;" he said, in broken English, "I suppose lowering gold;" he did not say, "Is it for lowering gold?" he said, "I suppose for lowering gold;" or "For lowering gold."
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating, that the watch-case in question was never made by him; that he had been a good deal absent from his ship and left the management to a foreman, who had now left him, and could not be found.
SAMUEL QUENDY. I was in the employ of Mr. Humbert, but since his death I have been employed by the prisoner - there was a workman in his shop of the name of Fotex - he worked there three weeks, and left a day or two after the apprehension of the prisoner; the prisoner's brother, who is here, told me of an order for a watch-case coming from Mr. Vulliamy's; I do not know whether it was executed; accidents often happen in finishing cases: the workmen often correct them without the master's knowledge; the prisoner was out collecting bills just before his apprehension; it was near quarter-day, and he had been out for several days, very often from nine o'clock in the morning; if not then, he went at three o'clock, after dinner; he was not particular to the hour of returning - generally when he went out, he left orders with his brother, whose business was to melt the gold - he had a brother, named Justine Mathey - he was an apprentice - in consequence of his doing work imperfectly, it was often necessary to re-melt the gold.
COURT. Q. Do you mean to say that the prisoner did not work at the business himself, and wear a workingdress when at home? A. No - he worked with his own hamls.
JUSTINE MATDEY. I am the prisoner's brother, and was employed as a workman by him. It was my department to melt the gold for cases; I remember the order for a gold watch-case for Mr. Vulliamy; I melted the gold on the 21st of June, and delivered it to Potex to make.
MR. LAW. Q. Did you deliver the gold bottom on the 27th, which had been stamped on the 23d? A. I do not know; I do not know whether that was sent to be stamped on the 23d.
COURT. Q. Are the Goldsmith's-hall to be marked, unless they are finished? A. No.
JOSEPH FORRESTER. I live in Princes-street, Bridgewater-square, and am a watch-maker. I gave orders to the prisoner, about the 20th of June, for a gold watch-case to be enamelled - it was for what we call a red enamel, and the gold ought to be of a high colour to take that enamel; I do not know that it was necessary to detach the rings from the case to make it of that colour; I saw a case at the prisoner's on the Saturday before he was apprehended; I understood it was the one made for me, but the rim was too pale; it was not of the colour for that enamel, and it was detached from the bottom; I saw it, but I never got my order from them; I never saw a case of that description before.
MR. LAW. Q. And what is there to prevent the rim and case being attached again? A. Nothing - they take the rim off and alter it, but I suppose it is put on again.
COURT. Q. I take it for granted you ordered the case of a particular description to have a red enamel? A. Yes- the rims I saw, I think had not been marked at the hall - the bottom had - there was a mark on the bottom.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Transported for Seven Years.
The prisoner, being a foreigner, had the evidence communicated to him by an interpreter.

2030. MARY ANN HARRIS was indicted for stealing, on the 22d of October, 4 shirts, value 20s.; 9 cravats, value 9s.; 6 handkerchiefs, value 4s.; 3 pairs of stockings, value 2s.; 2 towels, value 1s., and 1 night-cap, value 6d., the goods of Matthew Ford.
CHARLOTTE SUTTON. I am a laundress. On the 22d of October I packed up the articles, stated in the indictment, in a basket - they belong to Matthew Ford - I delivered them to Whitmore to take to Mr. Ford - the prisoner is a stranger.
JUDITH WHITMORE. I received this basket of clothes, and delivered it to Whitfield, Mr. Ford's housekeeper, at his house in Lincoln's-Inn-fields.
ANN WHITFIELD. I am housekeeper to Mr. Ford. - Whitmore delivered me this basket of linen - I put it into the bed-room, on the second floor, and missed it next morning (Sunday) when I got up - the prisoner is a a stranger, and had no business in the house - the street-door was double-locked.
FRANCIS SMITH. I am a watchman. On Sunday morning I saw the prisoner carrying a bundle in Lincoln's-Inn-fields; I asked what she was doing - she said she brought it from the mangle - I detained her - I think she was intoxicated.
WILLIAM BISHOP. I received the prisoner in charge with the property.
GUILTY. Aged 30.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. They were given to me in the street by rather a genteel man - I was not quite sober - he asked me to hold it.
Transported for Seven Years.

WILLIAM SUMMERS. I am an officer, and have the shirt - the prisoner bears a very good character.
MOSES NATHAN. I am a slopseller, and live in Ratcliff-highway - this shirt is mine.
